Figure <a href="#fig:forced-subtree-jitter" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:forced-subtree-jitter">2</a> illustrates the forced selection and the shared global offset produced by <a href="#eq:prefix-offset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-offset">[eq:prefix-offset]</a>–<a href="#eq:global-plateau" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:global-plateau">[eq:global-plateau]</a>.

<figure id="fig:forced-subtree-jitter">

<figcaption>The labels “odd” in panel (a) describe chosen-term roles. A singleton odd term, including at the root, is negligible after signed smoothing. For a remaining odd root term, selecting the first two active children recursively forces a binary subtree with $2^{b}$ selected leaves and hence $r ≥ 2^{b}$ ($b = 3$ shown). The selection is fixed by the term roles and sibling order. Panel (b) illustrates <a href="#eq:prefix-offset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-offset">[eq:prefix-offset]</a>–<a href="#eq:global-plateau" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:global-plateau">[eq:global-plateau]</a> for fixed distinct central sibling IDs $u_{1} < u_{2}$ in one group $G$. Conditional on the exact typical report $D_{0} = 𝔡$, the local sums $H_{I1}, H_{I2}$ and the global sum $V_{G}$ are independent; the total offsets $Y_{I1}, Y_{I2}$ share $V_{G}$ and are estimated jointly.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-forced-subtree-jitter"></a>

First sum the extra labels so that only the forced anchor expectation remains. Fix the forced labels and all nonleaf labels. For an assignment $\mathbf h$ of the $e$ extra leaf labels, let $a_{\mathbf h}$ be the nonnegative expectation in <a href="#eq:anchor-extra-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:anchor-extra-bound">[eq:anchor-extra-bound]</a>. At fixed bits each extra leaf has at most $L$ labels satisfying its constraint, even if several constraints share bits. Hence $$\sum_{\mathbf h}a_{\mathbf h}\le L^e\mathbb E_{\mathfrak d}f,
 \qquad
 \sum_{\mathbf h}a_{\mathbf h}^2
 \le\left(\sum_{\mathbf h}a_{\mathbf h}\right)^2
 \le L^{2e}(\mathbb E_{\mathfrak d}f)^2.$$ This avoids a free factor $T_1$ per extra leaf. At each positive nonroot level, there are at most $e$ occupied nonforced vertices: their descendant sets are disjoint at that level, and each contains an extra leaf and no forced leaf. Summing their child labels costs at most $(\prod_{j=2}^bT_j)^e$. With the forced labels fixed, $f$ does not depend on which nonforced labels were selected, because the complete tree and every group mechanism were fixed before choosing $\mathcal S$.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### The fixed-label anchor factorization

<a id="eq:outer-anchor-cost"></a>
It remains to sum $(\mathbb E_{\mathfrak d}f)^2$ over the forced labels. Call a forced sibling pair valid when its two labels are central and their positive difference is less than $L$. Dropping any further order restrictions involving nonforced children can only increase this nonnegative sum. Define the outer anchor convolution $$K_{\rm out}^{\mathfrak d}(z)=\mathbb E_{\mathfrak d}\chi(z-X).$$ Its $\ell^1$ mass is $L$, and its supremum is at most $O(L\omega^{-1}J^{-1/2})$ by the hidden root count. Including at most $2L$ choices for the close root child, its squared sum costs $$\label{eq:outer-anchor-cost}
 \sum_{\substack{u_1,u_2\text{ valid root}\\\text{forced labels}}}
       K_{\rm out}^{\mathfrak d}(u_1)^2
 \le \Gamma_{\rm out},\qquad
 \Gamma_{\rm out}=O(L^3\omega^{-1}J^{-1/2}).$$

For a fixed forced labeling, let $\mathcal G_j$ be its forced level-$(j+1)$ parents. Each $G\in\mathcal G_j$ has two forced level-$j$ children $Gu_1(G),Gu_2(G)$ with fixed distinct labels $u_1(G)<u_2(G)$ in the middle-half interval. For any such fixed $G$ and sibling labels $u_1<u_2$, define their joint anchor kernel by <a id="eq:joint-anchor"></a>$$\label{eq:joint-anchor}
 \mathcal H_{j,G;u_1,u_2}^{\mathfrak d}(z_1,z_2)
 =\mathbb E_{\mathfrak d}\left[
   \chi(z_1-w_j+Y_{Gu_1})\chi(z_2-w_j+Y_{Gu_2})\right].$$ The expectation here uses that group’s global bits and the two local collections under the fixed report. The actual $G,u_1,u_2$ select those collections; only $z_1,z_2$ are variables in the norm below. The factor can vary with all of these fixed data.

Independence of the distinct group mechanisms conditional on $D_0=\mathfrak d$ now gives, for this fixed forced labeling, <a id="eq:anchor-factorization"></a>$$\label{eq:anchor-factorization}
 \mathbb E_{\mathfrak d}f
 =K_{\rm out}^{\mathfrak d}(h_\varnothing)
   \prod_{j=1}^{b-1}\prod_{G\in\mathcal G_j}
   \mathcal H_{j,G;u_1(G),u_2(G)}^{\mathfrak d}
       (h_{Gu_1(G)},h_{Gu_2(G)}).$$ The group sets and sibling IDs in this identity are determined by the fixed upper labels. In particular $\mathcal G_{b-1}$ contains the root; its interior global/local mechanism is independent of the outer mechanism defining $X$.

For fixed distinct plateau siblings, <a href="#eq:global-plateau" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:global-plateau">[eq:global-plateau]</a> gives $Y_{Gu_1}=V_G+H_{Gu_1}$ and $Y_{Gu_2}=V_G+H_{Gu_2}$. Conditional on $\mathfrak d$, the two local hidden sums are independent of each other and of the shared global hidden sum, though their visible shifts and hidden counts can differ. We have uniformly in the fixed data <a id="eq:joint-anchor-norms"></a>$$\label{eq:joint-anchor-norms}
\begin{aligned}
 \|\mathcal H_{j,G;u_1,u_2}^{\mathfrak d}\|_{\ell^1(\mathbb Z^2)}&\le L^2,\\
 \|\mathcal H_{j,G;u_1,u_2}^{\mathfrak d}\|_{\ell^\infty(\mathbb Z^2)}
 &\le C_{10}L^2\omega^{-2}(T_{j+1}t_j)^{-1/2}t_j^{-1/2}.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $C_{10}$ is absolute. The first bound follows by summing the two indicators before averaging. For the supremum, first fix all global bits and bound the local average at $Gu_2$ by $O(L\omega^{-1}t_j^{-1/2})$. This bound is uniform in the global offset, so discard that factor’s constraint. Average the other factor over its local bits and the global bits. The latter contain at least a constant times $\omega^2T_{j+1}t_j$ hidden fair trials, giving $O(L\omega^{-1}(T_{j+1}t_j)^{-1/2})$. Multiplication proves the displayed joint bound without treating the two offsets as independent.

<a id="eq:group-anchor-cost"></a>
Multiplying the $\ell^1$ and supremum bounds controls the squared $\ell^2$ sum. Allowing a close child for each anchor costs at most $(2L)^2$. Thus for fixed valid $G,u_1,u_2$, $$\label{eq:group-anchor-cost}
\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\substack{z_1,z'_1,z_2,z'_2\in\mathbb Z\\
                  0<z'_1-z_1<L,\ 0<z'_2-z_2<L}}
       \mathcal H_{j,G;u_1,u_2}^{\mathfrak d}(z_1,z_2)^2
 &\le\Gamma_j,\\
 \Gamma_j&=C_{11}L^6\omega^{-2}T_{j+1}^{-1/2}t_j^{-1},
\end{aligned}$$ with an absolute $C_{11}$ large enough for every group.

<a id="eq:forced-anchor-sum"></a>
We make the order of the nested sums explicit. For two fixed valid level-$j$ siblings $Gu_1,Gu_2$, let $\mathcal R_j(G;u_1,u_2)$ be the sum of the squares of the products of all their interior $\mathcal H$ factors, including the factor of $G$, over all valid forced descendant labelings. Set $\mathcal R_0=1$. Splitting the descendant labels at their first two pairs gives the exact recurrence <a id="eq:anchor-sum-recursion"></a>$$\label{eq:anchor-sum-recursion}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal R_j(G;u_1,u_2)
 &=\sum_{\substack{(z_1,z'_1),(z_2,z'_2)\\
                    \text{valid at level }j}}
 \mathcal H_{j,G;u_1,u_2}^{\mathfrak d}(z_1,z_2)^2
 \prod_{v=1}^2\mathcal R_{j-1}(Gu_v;z_v,z'_v)\\
 &\le \Gamma_j\left(\sup_{G',v_1,v_2\text{ valid}}\mathcal R_{j-1}(G';v_1,v_2)\right)^2.
\end{aligned}$$ For the inequality, first use the uniform descendant bound on the valid distinct child pairs. Only afterward enlarge the remaining close-label sums to at most $2L$ choices and apply <a href="#eq:group-anchor-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:group-anchor-cost">[eq:group-anchor-cost]</a>. No group estimate is invoked at coincident sibling IDs. Iterating this inequality from the leaves upward, and finally using <a href="#eq:outer-anchor-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:outer-anchor-cost">[eq:outer-anchor-cost]</a>, gives $$\label{eq:forced-anchor-sum}
 \sum_{\text{valid forced labels}}(\mathbb E_{\mathfrak d}f)^2
 \le \Gamma_{\rm out}\prod_{j=1}^{b-1}\Gamma_j^{2^{b-j-1}}.$$ This procedure applies a uniform bound to each actual group after its upper IDs are fixed; it does not replace the varying kernels by one stationary kernel.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### The exponent gain and closure

Combine the extra-label reduction, <a href="#eq:forced-anchor-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:forced-anchor-sum">[eq:forced-anchor-sum]</a>, and the squared moment factor $(C_1r_i)^{2r}N^{-a_0dr}$. The fixed shape and expansion counts, the powers of $L$ and $\omega^{-1}$, and the positive factor $(C_1r_i)^{2r}$ contribute $N^{o(\epsilon)}$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:scale-margins" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scale-margins">3</a> and <a href="#eq:block-losses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-losses">[eq:block-losses]</a>. The squared degree contribution of a branching term is therefore at most $$N^{o(\epsilon)}J^{-1/2}
 \prod_{j=1}^{b-1}\bigl[T_{j+1}^{-1/2}t_j^{-1}\bigr]^{2^{b-j-1}}
 N^{-2^ba_0d}N^{-\lambda_de}.$$ The last factor pays for every extra leaf and is at most one by <a href="#eq:score-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:score-gap">[eq:score-gap]</a>. Using the lower bounds for $J,t_j$, the decay exponent of the remaining factors is at least $$\begin{aligned}
 &\epsilon/2+\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}2^{b-j-1}(\kappa_{j+1}/2+\epsilon)
       +2^ba_0d-o(\epsilon)\\
 &\qquad\ge d+(b-1)/4
       -\bigl[(2^b-1)d+3b\,2^{b-1}\bigr]\epsilon-o(\epsilon)
       >d'+.03.
\end{aligned}$$ For this calculation, <a href="#eq:local-exponents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-exponents">[eq:local-exponents]</a> gives $\kappa_{j+1}=2^{j-b}-(6\cdot2^j-2-2^{j-b})\epsilon$. Its leading contribution at depth $j$ is $2^{b-j-1}2^{j-b}/2=1/4$, which is the gain from the shared location of each forced sibling group. The bound $d\le2.20+(B/4)D$ and the chosen $\epsilon$ absorb the displayed corrections and leave the strict margin over $d'=d+(b-1)/4-.05$.

For each $r<r_*$, combine the fixed-shape estimates by Cauchy–Schwarz in <a href="#eq:degree-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degree-reduction">[eq:degree-reduction]</a>. The noncentral and singleton terms are at most $N^{-d'-1}$ in squared norm, and the branching terms have the margin just proved. Summing the finitely many degree norms and the tail in <a href="#eq:tree_high" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree_high">[eq:tree_high]</a> yields $$\|G_+-G_-\|_{r_i}^2\le N^{-d'-.01},$$ which proves Lemma <a href="#lem:tree-score" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tree-score">17</a>.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:clipping" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:clipping">8</a> to the preliminary laws $\widetilde\mu_i,\widetilde\nu_i$, their common report $D_0$, and the conditional densities <a href="#eq:conditional-densities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditional-densities">[eq:conditional-densities]</a>. The typical report has probability $1-o(\Delta)$, <a href="#eq:tree_high" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree_high">[eq:tree_high]</a> supplies $\rho=g_B$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:tree-score" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tree-score">17</a> supplies the density difference. Conditioning on the common event consisting of typical metadata and $G_+\ge1/2$ gives laws $\mu_i,\nu_i$ with the required domination and score. Each preliminary law changes by $o(\Delta)$. The hidden-bit law was used only before this conditioning.

Together with <a href="#eq:projection_tree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection_tree">[eq:projection_tree]</a>, the new conditioning and failed-layout loss is at most $\Delta$ for all large instances. The inherited loss is at most $(\prod_jT_j)E_{i-1}\le(\prod_jP_j)E_{i-1}=(\ell_i/\ell)E_{i-1}$. Thus one may take $$E_i\le\Delta+(\ell_i/\ell)E_{i-1},\qquad
 \frac{E_i}{\ell_i}\le\frac{\Delta}{\ell_i}
       +\frac{E_{i-1}}{\ell_{i-1}}\le(i+1)\Delta.$$ The length and overlap conditions were proved in Section <a href="#sec:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:block">5</a>, so this completes Proposition <a href="#prop:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:block">13</a>. Starting from Proposition <a href="#prop:initial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:initial">9</a> and iterating the block proves Proposition <a href="#prop:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:construction">5</a>.

<a id="sec:transfer"></a>
### Genuine traces, exact lengths, and testing

Proposition <a href="#prop:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:construction">5</a> now supplies the final pair and its auxiliary laws. We first retain a one-trace bound before using product observations. This proves the minimum-total-variation theorem independently of the sample-complexity deduction.

<a id="stmt-lem-restoration"></a>
<div id="lem:restoration" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:restored-product"></a>
<a id="eq:restored-single"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Restoration and padding). *For the parameters in Section <a href="#sec:scales" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:scales">2</a>, there are distinct words $x,y\in\{0,1\}^n$ such that, for every positive integer $m$, $$\label{eq:restored-product}
 \mathop{\mathrm{TV}}\bigl(\mathcal D_q(x)^{\otimes m},\mathcal D_q(y)^{\otimes m}\bigr)
 \le \sqrt{m n^{-d_D}}+2mn(D+1)\Delta.$$ In particular, $$\label{eq:restored-single}
 \mathop{\mathrm{TV}}\bigl(\mathcal D_q(x),\mathcal D_q(y)\bigr)
 \le n^{-d_D/2}+2n(D+1)\Delta.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-restoration"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-restoration</summary>

*Proof.* Append the same arbitrary binary tail to $x_D,y_D$ to reach length $n$. They remain distinct. Applying the final common oracle kernel, then generating an independent trace of the known tail and concatenating it, approximates each padded $\delta$-trace law within $E_D\le n(D+1)\Delta$ in total variation. Common padding by this kernel is the elementary monotonicity principle also used by McGregor, Price, and Vorotnikova (2014, proof of Lemma 10) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[MPV2014](#ref-MPV2014)].

The final moment estimate, monotonicity of norms on a probability space, and $r_D\ge2$ give $$H^2(\mu_D,\nu_D)
 \le\|d\nu_D/d\mu_D-1\|_{L^2(\mu_D)}^2
 \le n^{-d_D}.$$ Tensorization and $\mathop{\mathrm{TV}}\le H$ bound the oracle product distance by $\sqrt{m n^{-d_D}}$. Replacing each of the $m$ projected factors by its genuine law costs at most $mE_D$ for each hypothesis, by tensor telescoping. This proves <a href="#eq:restored-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:restored-product">[eq:restored-product]</a> for $\delta$. Applying the additional deletion kernel independently to each sample proves the same bound for the requested $q$. Taking $m=1$ gives <a href="#eq:restored-single" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:restored-single">[eq:restored-single]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-restoration-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-restoration</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:tv" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tv">2</a>.* Fix any $q\in(0,1)$. Choose $B=2$, $\zeta=1/2$, and auxiliary coefficients $c=1/(80\log2)<c_+=1/(40\log2)$ once, independently of $A$. The setup inequality holds because its left side is $1/(20\log2)$. The construction remains valid with these fixed parameters; its proof requires only their positivity and the stated eventual inequalities. In this case $\delta>0$ is fixed, $D\to\infty$, and $d_D=2.20+0.20D\to\infty$. For every fixed $A>0$, multiplying <a href="#eq:restored-single" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:restored-single">[eq:restored-single]</a> by $n^A$ gives $$n^{A-d_D/2}+2(D+1)n^{A+1-100(d_D+B+1)}\longrightarrow0.$$ The construction is available for every sufficiently large integer $n$, not only a subsequence; its pair has length at most $n$ and common padding reaches $n$ exactly. This proves <a href="#eq:min-tv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:min-tv">[eq:min-tv]</a>. The sample-complexity conclusion follows from Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, whose proof below does not use Theorem <a href="#thm:tv" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tv">2</a>. Indeed, $c\log(q^3\log n)\to\infty$ for any fixed admissible $c>0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### Two-point testing with a strict exponent margin

For two equally likely hypotheses with observation laws $P,Q$, every randomized decision rule has average success at most $(1+\mathop{\mathrm{TV}}(P,Q))/2$. If $\varphi$ is the conditional probability of choosing the first hypothesis, its average success is $$\frac12\bigl(1+\mathbb E_P\varphi-\mathbb E_Q\varphi\bigr)
 \le\frac12(1+\mathop{\mathrm{TV}}(P,Q)),\qquad 0\le\varphi\le1.$$ This is the standard two-point comparison; trace-reconstruction versions appear in Holden and Lyons (2020, Appendix A.2) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[HoldenLyons2020](#ref-HoldenLyons2020)].

<a id="eq:final-exponent"></a>
<a id="eq:intermediate-threshold"></a>
Fix $0<c<c_+<1/(4\log2)$ and choose $B,\zeta$ so that $$\alpha:=\frac{(1-\zeta)((B-1)/4-.05)}{B\log2}>c_+.$$ The $j_0$ preliminary blocks have fixed number depending only on $B$, and therefore $$\label{eq:final-exponent}
 d_D=\alpha\log H_{\rm del}+O_B(1).$$ For an absolute constant $a>0$ sufficiently small, <a href="#eq:restored-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:restored-product">[eq:restored-product]</a> with $m\le a n^{d_D}$ is strictly less than $1/3$ eventually: its first term is at most $\sqrt a$, and its second is at most $$2a(D+1)n^{d_D+1-100(d_D+B+1)}=o(1).$$ Thus the maximal average success for the pair is at most $2/3$ (in fact strictly less for all sufficiently large instances). Recall from the channel reduction in Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a> that $\log H_{\rm del}=\log(q^3\log n)+O(1)$ uniformly in $q$. The strict inequality $\alpha>c_+$ and <a href="#eq:final-exponent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-exponent">[eq:final-exponent]</a> therefore imply $$\label{eq:intermediate-threshold}
 a n^{d_D}\ge n^{c_+\log(q^3\log n)}$$ eventually. Exact worst-case reconstruction with success $2/3$ would distinguish this pair with that success, so this proves the usual $2/3$ lower bound, with the additional margin needed next.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Every fixed positive reconstruction success probability

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-restoration-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-restoration</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Fix $s\in(0,1]$ and $c$ as in the theorem, and retain $c_+>c$ above. Choose a fixed positive integer $g$ such that $(2/3)^g<s$. Put $N=\lfloor n/g\rfloor$ and construct the hard pair at length $N$ using the same requested deletion rate $q$ as the original instance. Its working rate is still $\delta=\min(q,1/2)$. Since $$q^3\log N\sim q^3\log n\to\infty,
 \qquad \log N=\log n+O_g(1),$$ the preceding construction and testing bounds apply to each gadget.

<a id="eq:gadget-margin"></a>
The exponent margin is preserved after dividing the length by $g$. More explicitly, $$\frac{(\log N)\log(q^3\log N)}
      {(\log n)\log(q^3\log n)}\longrightarrow1,$$ so $c_+>c$ gives $$\label{eq:gadget-margin}
 N^{c_+\log(q^3\log N)}
 \ge n^{c\log(q^3\log n)}$$ eventually. This is why the strict intermediate exponent was chosen before fixing the number of gadgets.

Take $g$ independent fair choices between the two length-$N$ words, concatenate the chosen words, and append a common known tail of length $n-gN$. This gives $2^g$ distinct length-$n$ hypotheses. Give the estimator the stronger observation in which every trace is separated at the gadget boundaries, together with an independent trace of the tail. Concatenation simulates the usual observation exactly.

The amplification of reconstruction difficulty using independent hard blocks also appears in McGregor, Price, and Vorotnikova (2014, sec. 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[MPV2014](#ref-MPV2014)] and Holden and Lyons (2020, sec. 4). For the fixed product prior above, the following posterior calculation justifies the multiplication of optimal success probabilities.

Under the product prior, the separated data from different gadgets are independent. Their posterior distributions factor as well: for a hypothesis vector $\theta=(\theta_1,\ldots,\theta_g)$ and separated data $z=(z_1,\ldots,z_g)$, $$\mathbb P(\theta\mid z)=\prod_{j=1}^g\mathbb P(\theta_j\mid z_j).$$ Hence the largest conditional probability of a correct joint guess is the product of the largest single-gadget posterior probabilities. Averaging, independence of the $z_j$ shows that the optimal joint success is the product of the optimal single-gadget average successes. The tail has the same law under every hypothesis and changes none of these posteriors.

For any positive integer sample budget $m\le n^{c\log(q^3\log n)}$, Equations <a href="#eq:intermediate-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intermediate-threshold">[eq:intermediate-threshold]</a> and <a href="#eq:gadget-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gadget-margin">[eq:gadget-margin]</a> bound every single-gadget success by $2/3$. The joint average success is therefore at most $(2/3)^g<s$. When $m=0$, the posterior remains the uniform prior, so the optimal joint success is $2^{-g}\le(2/3)^g<s$. A reconstruction procedure with worst-case success at least $s$ would have average success at least $s$ under this finite prior, a contradiction even in the stronger experiment. No nonnegative integer budget at most the claimed threshold suffices, which implies <a href="#eq:main-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-bound">[eq:main-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>
